Stage 1 · Courses 1 to 3 · about 1 month

Understanding AI and talking to models

  • AI: a program that imitates human intelligence. Machine learning: it learns from examples. Deep learning: it learns with multi-layer neural networks.
  • Generative AI: it creates text, images, sound or code. It guesses the most likely continuation, like a super game of "guess what comes next".
  • Natural language processing (NLP): teaching computers to understand and produce our language.
  • Responsible AI: bias, privacy, hallucinations, copyright.

Prompt engineering: writing clear instructions. The techniques from the course:

TechniqueSimple picture
Zero-shotAsk without an example
Few-shotShow 2 or 3 examples first
Chain-of-thoughtAsk it to think step by step
Tree-of-thoughtExplore several paths before choosing
Interview patternLet the AI ask you questions first
Stage 1 · Example

A well-built prompt

Role: you are a patient cooking teacher.
Task: explain how to make pancake batter.
Context: for a 7-year-old, in simple words.
Format: 5 numbered steps, one sentence each.
Think step by step before answering.

This prompt has a role, a task, context, a format, and asks for step-by-step reasoning. Change one part at a time to see what improves the answer.

Stage 1 · Practice

Exercise

  1. Take courses 1 to 3 on Coursera, with their in-browser labs.
  2. Write the same prompt as zero-shot, few-shot and chain-of-thought for a small maths problem. Compare the answers.
  3. Try the interview pattern: "Before helping me plan a trip, ask me 5 questions."
Stage 1 · Mini-quiz

Mini-quiz

1. You show the AI 3 examples before your question. Which technique?

Few-shot prompting.

2. Which technique asks the model to think step by step?

Chain-of-thought.

3. Order from broadest to narrowest: deep learning, AI, machine learning.

AI, then machine learning, then deep learning.
Stage 2 · Courses 4 and 5 · about 1 month

Learning Python and building a web app

Python is the most used language for working with AI models. You learn:

  • Variables (labelled boxes), lists and dictionaries (boxes with named drawers).
  • Conditions (if… else) and loops (doing the same thing again).
  • Functions: a reusable recipe.
  • NumPy and pandas: tools for numbers and tables.
  • APIs: asking another program for data over the internet.

Flask is a small tool to build a website in Python. You also learn unit tests (automatically checking your code works) and deployment (putting your app online).

Stage 2 · Example

Your first Flask app

from flask import Flask, request

app = Flask(__name__)

@app.route("/hello")
def hello():
    name = request.args.get("name", "friend")
    return f"Hello, {name}!"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run(debug=True)

Run the program, then open http://127.0.0.1:5000/hello?name=Alain in your browser. The page answers "Hello, Alain!". Later, instead of "Hello", an AI model will answer here.

Stage 2 · Practice

Exercise

  1. Take courses 4 and 5 with all their labs (the longest part of this stage).
  2. Write a Python function that counts the words in a sentence, and a unit test for it.
  3. Build the Flask app from the previous slide and add a /count?text=... page.
  4. Put your code on GitHub (review the GitHub Foundations course if needed).
Stage 2 · Mini-quiz

Mini-quiz

1. What is Flask for?

Building a web app in Python, with pages (routes) that answer requests.

2. What is a unit test?

A small program that automatically checks that part of your code gives the right result.

3. Which Python library is used to handle data tables?

pandas (and NumPy for number crunching).
Stage 3 · Courses 6 to 8 · about 6 weeks

First AI apps, data and machine learning

Course 6: you build real generative AI apps (chatbot, summariser, transcription) with Hugging Face (a big library of free models), LangChain and a small interface (Gradio).

Course 7, data analysis: clean the data (missing values, duplicates), explore it with charts, find relationships. Like tidying your room before looking for a toy.

Course 8, machine learning with scikit-learn:

FamilyWhat you predictExample
RegressionA numberA house price
ClassificationA categorySpam or not spam
ClusteringGroups, with no known answerCustomer families

You always split data into training and test sets, to check the model on examples it has never seen.

Stage 3 · Example

A model in 6 lines

from sklearn.datasets import load_iris
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier

X, y = load_iris(return_X_y=True)                 # flowers and their species
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.2)
model = DecisionTreeClassifier().fit(X_train, y_train)   # learn
print("Accuracy:", model.score(X_test, y_test))          # test

The model learns to recognise a flower's species from the size of its petals. 20 % of the flowers are kept aside for an honest test.

Stage 3 · Practice

Exercise

  1. Take courses 6 to 8 with their labs.
  2. Run the example from the previous slide. Change test_size and watch the accuracy.
  3. Build a small chatbot with a free Hugging Face model and a Gradio interface.
Stage 3 · Mini-quiz

Mini-quiz

1. You want to predict tomorrow's temperature. Regression or classification?

Regression, because you predict a number.

2. Why keep test data separate?

To check the model on examples it has never seen, and to detect overfitting.

3. What is Hugging Face?

A platform and libraries giving access to thousands of AI models, often free.
Stage 4 · Courses 9 to 12 · about 1 month

Inside large language models

  • The neural network: layers of small calculators linked together. Each link has a weight that training adjusts little by little. With Keras, then PyTorch.
  • Tokens: text cut into small pieces. Tokenisation is the first step.
  • Embeddings: each word becomes a list of numbers. Words with similar meaning have similar numbers ("king" is near "queen").
  • Attention: the model looks at which words matter to each other. In "The cat drank its milk because it was thirsty", attention links "it" to "cat".
  • The transformer: the architecture made of attention layers. Two big families: encoders (like BERT, for understanding) and decoders (like GPT, for writing).
  • Data preparation: cleaning, tokenising and loading texts with PyTorch tools (DataLoader).
Stage 4 · Example

Seeing tokens and embeddings

from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModel
import torch

name = "bert-base-uncased"
tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(name)
model = AutoModel.from_pretrained(name)

inputs = tok("The cat drinks milk", return_tensors="pt")
print(tok.convert_ids_to_tokens(inputs["input_ids"][0]))   # the tokens
with torch.no_grad():
    out = model(**inputs)
print(out.last_hidden_state.shape)   # a vector of 768 numbers per token

You see the text cut into tokens, then each token turned into a list of 768 numbers describing its meaning in the sentence.

Stage 4 · Practice

Exercise

  1. Take courses 9 to 12. Don't get stuck on formulas: understand the idea first, then the code.
  2. Run the example from the previous slide with a sentence of your choice.
  3. Draw a transformer by hand: tokens → embeddings → attention layers → next-word prediction.
Stage 4 · Mini-quiz

Mini-quiz

1. What is the attention mechanism for?

Finding which words in a sentence matter to each other, to understand the meaning.

2. Is BERT an encoder or a decoder?

An encoder: it is mainly for understanding. GPT is a decoder, built for writing.

3. What does training change in a neural network?

The weights of the links between neurons.
Stage 5 · Courses 13 and 14 · about 3 weeks

Adapting a model to your needs

Fine-tuning: take an already trained model and give it private lessons with your own examples. Like a cook who can already cook and learns your family recipes.

  • Full fine-tuning: all weights are adjusted. Very effective, but very costly in memory and time.
  • PEFT (parameter-efficient fine-tuning): only a small part is adjusted. The best-known method is LoRA, which adds small extra pieces to the model instead of changing everything. QLoRA does the same with a compressed model to save memory.
  • Instruction tuning: teaching the model to follow instructions, with "question → right answer" pairs.
  • Aligning with human preferences: RLHF (reinforcement learning from human feedback: people rate answers) and DPO (a simpler method that learns directly from "good answer / bad answer" pairs).
Before fine-tuning, ask whether a better prompt or RAG (stage 6) would be enough. It's often cheaper.
Stage 5 · Example

LoRA in a few lines

from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM
from peft import LoraConfig, get_peft_model

model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("gpt2")
config = LoraConfig(r=8, lora_alpha=16, target_modules=["c_attn"],
                    lora_dropout=0.05, task_type="CAUSAL_LM")
model = get_peft_model(model, config)
model.print_trainable_parameters()   # less than 1 % of the weights are trained

The model keeps its knowledge, and only small LoRA pieces learn your examples. That's why LoRA fits on a modest computer.

Stage 5 · Practice

Exercise

  1. Take courses 13 and 14 and their labs (they provide machines with a graphics card).
  2. Run the LoRA example and note the percentage of trainable weights.
  3. Write 3 sentences on the difference between full fine-tuning, LoRA and RAG.
Stage 5 · Mini-quiz

Mini-quiz

1. Why use LoRA rather than full fine-tuning?

Because it trains only a small part of the weights: much less memory, time and money.

2. People rate the model's answers to improve it. What is this method called?

RLHF (reinforcement learning from human feedback).

3. Which method learns directly from "good answer / bad answer" pairs, without a separate reward model?

DPO (Direct Preference Optimization).
Stage 6 · Courses 15 and 16 · about 3 weeks

RAG, agents and your final project

RAG: before answering, the model looks up the right passages in your documents. An open-book exam.

  1. Load the documents (PDFs, web pages).
  2. Cut them into chunks.
  3. Turn each chunk into an embedding.
  4. Store them in a vector database (Chroma or FAISS in the course).
  5. For each question: find the closest chunks and give them to the model with the question.

LangChain snaps these steps together like LEGO bricks. Agents go further: the model decides by itself which tools to call (a search, a calculator, an API) to finish a task.

The final project (course 16): a question-answering app over documents, with RAG, LangChain and a Gradio interface. Keep it for your portfolio.

Stage 6 · Example

A mini RAG

from langchain_community.vectorstores import Chroma
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings
from langchain_text_splitters import RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter

text = open("rules.txt", encoding="utf-8").read()
chunks = RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter(chunk_size=500, chunk_overlap=50).split_text(text)
db = Chroma.from_texts(chunks, HuggingFaceEmbeddings())
hits = db.similarity_search("How many days off?", k=3)
context = "\n".join(d.page_content for d in hits)
# Then send to the LLM: "Answer using this context:" + context + the question

The exact LangChain package names change often: follow the version used in the course labs.

Stage 6 · Practice

Exercise and project

  1. Take course 15, then the course 16 project.
  2. Build your RAG over a real document you care about (for example the AWS or GitHub exam guide).
  3. Test 10 questions. When an answer is wrong, note whether the search failed or the model did.
  4. Publish the code on GitHub with a clear README and a screenshot.
Stage 6 · Final quiz

Final quiz (mixed)

1. In RAG, what is the vector database for?

Storing the embeddings of document chunks so the ones most similar to the question can be found quickly.

2. Your chatbot must answer from documents that change every week. RAG or fine-tuning?

RAG: you just update the documents, no retraining.

3. What's the difference between a LangChain chain and an agent?

A chain follows steps fixed in advance. An agent chooses by itself which tools to use and in what order.

4. Which lightweight method adapts a model by training less than 1 % of its weights?

LoRA (a PEFT method).

5. Which library quickly builds a small web interface to test a model?

Gradio (or Flask for a real web app).
To finish

What it costs, and my honest opinion

  • The price. It goes through a monthly Coursera subscription (or Coursera Plus). The certificate page gives no exact price: Coursera certificates often cost around 49 USD a month in the US, but check for your country. Over 6 months, count on about 300 USD. Financial aid is available. There's no separate exam fee.
  • It is by far the hardest programme on this list. It needs real hours of coding. It's also the only one that teaches you to build AI apps, not just choose them.
  • Value. The certificate itself carries little weight: it is not a proctored exam. What counts is the RAG project published on GitHub that you can show in an interview.
  • The content ages. LangChain and the tools change fast, and parts of the courses may be a bit dated. The ideas (transformers, fine-tuning, RAG) stay solid.
  • My advice: don't start it at the same time as another certification. Do it after AWS AI Practitioner, and only if you want to become an AI developer.
To finish

  • Coursera, IBM Generative AI Engineering Professional Certificate (16 courses, length, tools, ACE credits)
  • Hugging Face Learn (free complementary courses)
